Once you stop fighting for a "globally unique" implementation and instead leave a generic slot on the trait to scope uniqueness to a local context, bypassing the Orphan Rule takes no black magic at all. It is just a simple shift in perspective, yet it makes both library design and large workspace boundaries much cleaner.In the next post, I will talk about the problem that immediately follows this design: once your traits and query builders carry a generic context parameter, how do you avoid falling into generic type gymnastics and monomorphization bloat? That brings us to boundary validation and type erasure.

At this point, you might think that dropping a local type MyApp anywhere into a trait's generic list solves the Orphan Rule for good. Most of the time, it does. But if your trait has more than one generic parameter, or if you want to write a generic impl, there is an ordering restriction that is easy to trip over.When the compiler runs its Orphan Rule check, it scans the types in a fixed order: first the Self type, then the trait's 1st generic parameter, then the 2nd, and so on. The rule comes down to one sentence: no bare generic type parameter T may appear before the first local type.Code makes this clearest. Suppose an external crate defines ForeignTrait and ForeignType, and you define MyApp locally:// ✅ Allowed: scans ForeignType, then hits local MyApp first; the trailing T is unrestricted impl ForeignTrait for ForeignType {} // ❌ Error: hits a bare T before seeing MyApp impl ForeignTrait for ForeignType {} // ❌ Error: hits a bare T right away in Self, before MyApp gets a chance impl ForeignTrait for T {}The only word to pay attention to here is "bare". In Rust terminology, this is called an uncovered type parameter. A plain T is uncovered. If it is wrapped inside a non-fundamental concrete type like Vec (built-in fundamental types like &T and Box do not count), it is no longer uncovered, so it will not block a local MyApp that comes after it:// ✅ Allowed: T is covered by Vec, so it is not an uncovered type parameter impl ForeignTrait, MyApp> for ForeignType {}From a library author's perspective, this means it is not enough to just add a generic slot; you also need to put it in the right place. If your trait has multiple generic parameters and there is no strong reason otherwise, put the context Ctx in the first type parameter position. That way, downstream users writing generic implementations can avoid most of the surprising Orphan Rule errors.A Truly Standalone models CrateOnce this mechanism clicks, you will see that it also solves a stubborn architectural headache in larger Rust codebases.As a project grows, it usually gets split into a Cargo workspace: maybe an API gateway crate, a few business service crates, and a background worker crate. These modules often share the same data structures, so the natural move is to pull those structs out into a standalone models crate.Traditionally, doing this in Rust is painful. Suppose models defines a Product struct with Uuid, Url, and DateTime fields. If your serialization library or ORM expects those field types to already implement its traits inside models, you are left with two bad options:Wrap everything in NewTypes inside models, and pull in the serialization library, database driver, and other heavy dependencies along with them. A crate that was supposed to hold plain data structures ends up with the heaviest dependency tree in the workspace.Give up on splitting it out, and dump models and business logic into the same crate. It saves time early on, but dependency boundaries and compile times get harder to manage as the project grows.With the generic Ctx parameter, that knot comes untied. Inside your standalone models crate, you just define your structs with native types:// crate: models pub struct Product { pub id: uuid::Uuid, pub created_at: chrono::DateTime, }Then you write a generic implementation for Product open to any Ctx (or let a derive macro generate it):impl ToJson for Product where uuid::Uuid: ToJson, chrono::DateTime: ToJson { fn to_json(&self) -> String { format!( "{{\"id\":{},\"created_at\":{}}}", ToJson::::to_json(&self.id), ToJson::::to_json(&self.created_at), ) } }Notice the subtle part here: inside the models crate, Uuid and DateTime do not implement ToJson yet, and this code still compiles.That is because models is only declaring a deferred contract: "whoever uses this later, as long as your context Ctx knows how to handle Uuid and DateTime, Product automatically supports ToJson under your context." As a result, models stays completely lightweight, carries no concrete conversion logic, and compiles very fast. The actual conversions are deferred to the downstream service crates, where each service hooks up what it needs using its own local context.For large workspaces, this keeps dependency boundaries clean. Models can evolve on their own, while different services remain free to pick whatever storage format or API representation they need.Real-World Example: How This Works in KovaThe ToJson snippet above is as minimal as it gets. Does this idea still hold up when you bring it into a real, more complex project?When building my own Rust ORM, Kova, I applied this pattern across the entire type system. It is a good concrete example of how this plays out in practice.ORMs are where the Orphan Rule hurts the most. A single table often mixes Uuid, Url, DateTime, HashMap, and custom domain types. Kova's core Encode and Decode traits use the exact generic slot design we just walked through:pub trait Encode<'a, X = ()> { fn encode(self) -> Result>; } pub trait Decode: Sized { fn decode(value: Value<'_>) -> Result; }Because Kova hands the extension point to the user, it only needs built-in support for basic database primitives: integers, floats, booleans, strings, and byte arrays. Its Cargo.toml does not have to maintain a pile of optional dependencies and feature flags for uuid, chrono, jiff, url, and so on.In a real application, the workflow looks just like the earlier example.First, define your table structs with native types in a standalone models crate and add #[derive(Model)]:// crate: models (no database driver or codec code needed) #[derive(Model)] pub struct Product { pub id: Uuid, pub price: Decimal, pub vendor_url: Url, pub created_at: DateTime, }#[derive(Model)] expands into an open contract like impl Model for Product where ..., so models compiles completely on its own without tying itself to any concrete codec implementation.Next, in your downstream service crate, define a local marker struct pub struct AppCodec; and implement zero-copy conversions matching your storage layout:pub struct AppCodec; // Store Uuid as 16 raw bytes, borrowing directly with zero copies impl<'a, 'b: 'a> Encode<'a, AppCodec> for &'b Uuid { fn encode(self) -> Result> { Ok(Value::Blob(Cow::Borrowed(self.as_bytes()))) } } // Store DateTime as an integer millisecond timestamp impl<'a> Encode<'a, AppCodec> for DateTime { fn encode(self) -> Result> { Ok(Value::Int8(self.timestamp_millis())) } }Finally, switch the client's context marker when you need it:// Default client using the () codec let client = Options::from(conn_str).build()?; // Switch to the local AppCodec at zero runtime cost let client = client.codec::();Switching the type carries zero runtime overhead; under the hood, it only swaps a PhantomData marker on Client from () to AppCodec. And while building Kova, another nice benefit showed up: once you have a local type like AppCodec, you can attach much more to it than just encoding and decoding.For example, suppose you have implemented Encode for url::Url in an ORM. If you still can't run LIKE queries on a Url column, or compare a Url column directly against a &str, that type still feels second-class in queries.So Kova puts the same generic extension slot on its SQL capability traits and type compatibility rules, such as CanString, CanMath, and Compatible. Users can add a couple of short declarations under their own AppCodec:// Give Url string capabilities (enabling methods like `like` and `ilike`) impl CanString for Url {} // Allow passing &str directly to a Url column without calling Url::parse() every time impl<'a> Compatible, AppCodec> for Static<&'a str> {}With that in place, third-party types fit right into the ORM's DSL and behave no differently from built-in types:client .update::() .set(Product::vendor_url.set("https://rust-lang.org")) .filter( Product::id.eq(&target_uuid) & Product::vendor_url.ilike("https://%") ) .execute() .await?;Putting this design through a real codebase like Kova confirmed something practical: opening up type conversions and capabilities to a user-defined context does more than just get around the Orphan Rule on paper. Even in a complex framework, it keeps dependency boundaries clean, compile times fast, and day-to-day usage comfortable.Rust's Orphan Rule is not there to make life hard. Even in a complex framework, it keeps dependency boundaries clean, compile times fast, and day-to-day usage comfortable.Rust's Orphan Rule is not there to make life hard. It exists to prevent global implementation conflicts and stop crates from stepping on each other in the global namespace. Once you stop fighting for a "globally unique" implementation and instead leave a generic slot on the trait to scope uniqueness to a local context, bypassing the Orphan Rule takes no black magic at all. It is just a simple shift in perspective, yet it makes both library design and large workspace boundaries much cleaner.In the next post, I will talk about the problem that immediately follows this design: once your traits and query builders carry a generic context parameter, how do you avoid falling into generic type gymnastics and monomorphization bloat? That brings us to boundary validation and type erasure.

From a library author's perspective, this means it is not enough to just add a generic slot; you also need to put it in the right place. If your trait has multiple generic parameters and there is no strong reason otherwise, put the context Ctx in the first type parameter position. That way, downstream users writing generic implementations can avoid most of the surprising Orphan Rule errors.A Truly Standalone models CrateOnce this mechanism clicks, you will see that it also solves a stubborn architectural headache in larger Rust codebases.As a project grows, it usually gets split into a Cargo workspace: maybe an API gateway crate, a few business service crates, and a background worker crate. These modules often share the same data structures, so the natural move is to pull those structs out into a standalone models crate.Traditionally, doing this in Rust is painful. Suppose models defines a Product struct with Uuid, Url, and DateTime fields. If your serialization library or ORM expects those field types to already implement its traits inside models, you are left with two bad options:Wrap everything in NewTypes inside models, and pull in the serialization library, database driver, and other heavy dependencies along with them. A crate that was supposed to hold plain data structures ends up with the heaviest dependency tree in the workspace.Give up on splitting it out, and dump models and business logic into the same crate. It saves time early on, but dependency boundaries and compile times get harder to manage as the project grows.With the generic Ctx parameter, that knot comes untied. Inside your standalone models crate, you just define your structs with native types:// crate: models pub struct Product { pub id: uuid::Uuid, pub created_at: chrono::DateTime, }Then you write a generic implementation for Product open to any Ctx (or let a derive macro generate it):impl ToJson for Product where uuid::Uuid: ToJson, chrono::DateTime: ToJson { fn to_json(&self) -> String { format!( "{{\"id\":{},\"created_at\":{}}}", ToJson::::to_json(&self.id), ToJson::::to_json(&self.created_at), ) } }Notice the subtle part here: inside the models crate, Uuid and DateTime do not implement ToJson yet, and this code still compiles.That is because models is only declaring a deferred contract: "whoever uses this later, as long as your context Ctx knows how to handle Uuid and DateTime, Product automatically supports ToJson under your context." As a result, models stays completely lightweight, carries no concrete conversion logic, and compiles very fast. The actual conversions are deferred to the downstream service crates, where each service hooks up what it needs using its own local context.For large workspaces, this keeps dependency boundaries clean. Models can evolve on their own, while different services remain free to pick whatever storage format or API representation they need.Real-World Example: How This Works in KovaThe ToJson snippet above is as minimal as it gets. Does this idea still hold up when you bring it into a real, more complex project?When building my own Rust ORM, Kova, I applied this pattern across the entire type system. It is a good concrete example of how this plays out in practice.ORMs are where the Orphan Rule hurts the most. A single table often mixes Uuid, Url, DateTime, HashMap, and custom domain types. Kova's core Encode and Decode traits use the exact generic slot design we just walked through:pub trait Encode<'a, X = ()> { fn encode(self) -> Result>; } pub trait Decode: Sized { fn decode(value: Value<'_>) -> Result; }Because Kova hands the extension point to the user, it only needs built-in support for basic database primitives: integers, floats, booleans, strings, and byte arrays. Its Cargo.toml does not have to maintain a pile of optional dependencies and feature flags for uuid, chrono, jiff, url, and so on.In a real application, the workflow looks just like the earlier example.First, define your table structs with native types in a standalone models crate and add #[derive(Model)]:// crate: models (no database driver or codec code needed) #[derive(Model)] pub struct Product { pub id: Uuid, pub price: Decimal, pub vendor_url: Url, pub created_at: DateTime, }#[derive(Model)] expands into an open contract like impl Model for Product where ..., so models compiles completely on its own without tying itself to any concrete codec implementation.Next, in your downstream service crate, define a local marker struct pub struct AppCodec; and implement zero-copy conversions matching your storage layout:pub struct AppCodec; // Store Uuid as 16 raw bytes, borrowing directly with zero copies impl<'a, 'b: 'a> Encode<'a, AppCodec> for &'b Uuid { fn encode(self) -> Result> { Ok(Value::Blob(Cow::Borrowed(self.as_bytes()))) } } // Store DateTime as an integer millisecond timestamp impl<'a> Encode<'a, AppCodec> for DateTime { fn encode(self) -> Result> { Ok(Value::Int8(self.timestamp_millis())) } }Finally, switch the client's context marker when you need it:// Default client using the () codec let client = Options::from(conn_str).build()?; // Switch to the local AppCodec at zero runtime cost let client = client.codec::();Switching the type carries zero runtime overhead; under the hood, it only swaps a PhantomData marker on Client from () to AppCodec. And while building Kova, another nice benefit showed up: once you have a local type like AppCodec, you can attach much more to it than just encoding and decoding.For example, suppose you have implemented Encode for url::Url in an ORM. If you still can't run LIKE queries on a Url column, or compare a Url column directly against a &str, that type still feels second-class in queries.So Kova puts the same generic extension slot on its SQL capability traits and type compatibility rules, such as CanString, CanMath, and Compatible. Users can add a couple of short declarations under their own AppCodec:// Give Url string capabilities (enabling methods like `like` and `ilike`) impl CanString for Url {} // Allow passing &str directly to a Url column without calling Url::parse() every time impl<'a> Compatible, AppCodec> for Static<&'a str> {}With that in place, third-party types fit right into the ORM's DSL and behave no differently from built-in types:client .update::() .set(Product::vendor_url.set("https://rust-lang.org")) .filter( Product::id.eq(&target_uuid) & Product::vendor_url.ilike("https://%") ) .execute() .await?;Putting this design through a real codebase like Kova confirmed something practical: opening up type conversions and capabilities to a user-defined context does more than just get around the Orphan Rule on paper. Even in a complex framework, it keeps dependency boundaries clean, compile times fast, and day-to-day usage comfortable.Rust's Orphan Rule is not there to make life hard. Once you stop fighting for a "globally unique" implementation and instead leave a generic slot on the trait to scope uniqueness to a local context, bypassing the Orphan Rule takes no black magic at all. Its Cargo.toml does not have to maintain a pile of optional dependencies and feature flags for uuid, chrono, jiff, url, and so on.In a real application, the workflow looks just like the earlier example.First, define your table structs with native types in a standalone models crate and add #[derive(Model)]:// crate: models (no database driver or codec code needed) #[derive(Model)] pub struct Product { pub id: Uuid, pub price: Decimal, pub vendor_url: Url, pub created_at: DateTime, }#[derive(Model)] expands into an open contract like impl Model for Product where ..., so models compiles completely on its own without tying itself to any concrete codec implementation.Next, in your downstream service crate, define a local marker struct pub struct AppCodec; and implement zero-copy conversions matching your storage layout:pub struct AppCodec; // Store Uuid as 16 raw bytes, borrowing directly with zero copies impl<'a, 'b: 'a> Encode<'a, AppCodec> for &'b Uuid { fn encode(self) -> Result> { Ok(Value::Blob(Cow::Borrowed(self.as_bytes()))) } } // Store DateTime as an integer millisecond timestamp impl<'a> Encode<'a, AppCodec> for DateTime { fn encode(self) -> Result> { Ok(Value::Int8(self.timestamp_millis())) } }Finally, switch the client's context marker when you need it:// Default client using the () codec let client = Options::from(conn_str).build()?; // Switch to the local AppCodec at zero runtime cost let client = client.codec::();Switching the type carries zero runtime overhead; under the hood, it only swaps a PhantomData marker on Client from () to AppCodec. And while building Kova, another nice benefit showed up: once you have a local type like AppCodec, you can attach much more to it than just encoding and decoding.For example, suppose you have implemented Encode for url::Url in an ORM. If you still can't run LIKE queries on a Url column, or compare a Url column directly against a &str, that type still feels second-class in queries.So Kova puts the same generic extension slot on its SQL capability traits and type compatibility rules, such as CanString, CanMath, and Compatible. Users can add a couple of short declarations under their own AppCodec:// Give Url string capabilities (enabling methods like `like` and `ilike`) impl CanString for Url {} // Allow passing &str directly to a Url column without calling Url::parse() every time impl<'a> Compatible, AppCodec> for Static<&'a str> {}With that in place, third-party types fit right into the ORM's DSL and behave no differently from built-in types:client .update::() .set(Product::vendor_url.set("https://rust-lang.org")) .filter( Product::id.eq(&target_uuid) & Product::vendor_url.ilike("https://%") ) .execute() .await?;Putting this design through a real codebase like Kova confirmed something practical: opening up type conversions and capabilities to a user-defined context does more than just get around the Orphan Rule on paper. Even in a complex framework, it keeps dependency boundaries clean, compile times fast, and day-to-day usage comfortable.Rust's Orphan Rule is not there to make life hard. It exists to prevent global implementation conflicts and stop crates from stepping on each other in the global namespace. Once you stop fighting for a "globally unique" implementation and instead leave a generic slot on the trait to scope uniqueness to a local context, bypassing the Orphan Rule takes no black magic at all. It is just a simple shift in perspective, yet it makes both library design and large workspace boundaries much cleaner.In the next post, I will talk about the problem that immediately follows this design: once your traits and query builders carry a generic context parameter, how do you avoid falling into generic type gymnastics and monomorphization bloat? That brings us to boundary validation and type erasure.

Then you write a generic implementation for Product open to any Ctx (or let a derive macro generate it):impl ToJson for Product where uuid::Uuid: ToJson, chrono::DateTime: ToJson { fn to_json(&self) -> String { format!( "{{\"id\":{},\"created_at\":{}}}", ToJson::::to_json(&self.id), ToJson::::to_json(&self.created_at), ) } }Notice the subtle part here: inside the models crate, Uuid and DateTime do not implement ToJson yet, and this code still compiles.That is because models is only declaring a deferred contract: "whoever uses this later, as long as your context Ctx knows how to handle Uuid and DateTime, Product automatically supports ToJson under your context." As a result, models stays completely lightweight, carries no concrete conversion logic, and compiles very fast. The actual conversions are deferred to the downstream service crates, where each service hooks up what it needs using its own local context.For large workspaces, this keeps dependency boundaries clean. Models can evolve on their own, while different services remain free to pick whatever storage format or API representation they need.Real-World Example: How This Works in KovaThe ToJson snippet above is as minimal as it gets. Users can add a couple of short declarations under their own AppCodec:// Give Url string capabilities (enabling methods like `like` and `ilike`) impl CanString for Url {} // Allow passing &str directly to a Url column without calling Url::parse() every time impl<'a> Compatible, AppCodec> for Static<&'a str> {}With that in place, third-party types fit right into the ORM's DSL and behave no differently from built-in types:client .update::() .set(Product::vendor_url.set("https://rust-lang.org")) .filter( Product::id.eq(&target_uuid) & Product::vendor_url.ilike("https://%") ) .execute() .await?;Putting this design through a real codebase like Kova confirmed something practical: opening up type conversions and capabilities to a user-defined context does more than just get around the Orphan Rule on paper. Even in a complex framework, it keeps dependency boundaries clean, compile times fast, and day-to-day usage comfortable.Rust's Orphan Rule is not there to make life hard. It exists to prevent global implementation conflicts and stop crates from stepping on each other in the global namespace. Once you stop fighting for a "globally unique" implementation and instead leave a generic slot on the trait to scope uniqueness to a local context, bypassing the Orphan Rule takes no black magic at all. It is just a simple shift in perspective, yet it makes both library design and large workspace boundaries much cleaner.In the next post, I will talk about the problem that immediately follows this design: once your traits and query builders carry a generic context parameter, how do you avoid falling into generic type gymnastics and monomorphization bloat? That brings us to boundary validation and type erasure.

ORMs are where the Orphan Rule hurts the most. A single table often mixes Uuid, Url, DateTime, HashMap, and custom domain types. Kova's core Encode and Decode traits use the exact generic slot design we just walked through:pub trait Encode<'a, X = ()> { fn encode(self) -> Result>; } pub trait Decode: Sized { fn decode(value: Value<'_>) -> Result; }Because Kova hands the extension point to the user, it only needs built-in support for basic database primitives: integers, floats, booleans, strings, and byte arrays. Its Cargo.toml does not have to maintain a pile of optional dependencies and feature flags for uuid, chrono, jiff, url, and so on.In a real application, the workflow looks just like the earlier example.First, define your table structs with native types in a standalone models crate and add #[derive(Model)]:// crate: models (no database driver or codec code needed) #[derive(Model)] pub struct Product { pub id: Uuid, pub price: Decimal, pub vendor_url: Url, pub created_at: DateTime, }#[derive(Model)] expands into an open contract like impl Model for Product where ..., so models compiles completely on its own without tying itself to any concrete codec implementation.Next, in your downstream service crate, define a local marker struct pub struct AppCodec; and implement zero-copy conversions matching your storage layout:pub struct AppCodec; // Store Uuid as 16 raw bytes, borrowing directly with zero copies impl<'a, 'b: 'a> Encode<'a, AppCodec> for &'b Uuid { fn encode(self) -> Result> { Ok(Value::Blob(Cow::Borrowed(self.as_bytes()))) } } // Store DateTime as an integer millisecond timestamp impl<'a> Encode<'a, AppCodec> for DateTime { fn encode(self) -> Result> { Ok(Value::Int8(self.timestamp_millis())) } }Finally, switch the client's context marker when you need it:// Default client using the () codec let client = Options::from(conn_str).build()?; // Switch to the local AppCodec at zero runtime cost let client = client.codec::();Switching the type carries zero runtime overhead; under the hood, it only swaps a PhantomData marker on Client from () to AppCodec. And while building Kova, another nice benefit showed up: once you have a local type like AppCodec, you can attach much more to it than just encoding and decoding.For example, suppose you have implemented Encode for url::Url in an ORM. Even in a complex framework, it keeps dependency boundaries clean, compile times fast, and day-to-day usage comfortable.Rust's Orphan Rule is not there to make life hard. It exists to prevent global implementation conflicts and stop crates from stepping on each other in the global namespace. Once you stop fighting for a "globally unique" implementation and instead leave a generic slot on the trait to scope uniqueness to a local context, bypassing the Orphan Rule takes no black magic at all. It is just a simple shift in perspective, yet it makes both library design and large workspace boundaries much cleaner.In the next post, I will talk about the problem that immediately follows this design: once your traits and query builders carry a generic context parameter, how do you avoid falling into generic type gymnastics and monomorphization bloat? That brings us to boundary validation and type erasure.

Rust's Orphan Rule is not there to make life hard. It exists to prevent global implementation conflicts and stop crates from stepping on each other in the global namespace. Once you stop fighting for a "globally unique" implementation and instead leave a generic slot on the trait to scope uniqueness to a local context, bypassing the Orphan Rule takes no black magic at all. It is just a simple shift in perspective, yet it makes both library design and large workspace boundaries much cleaner.In the next post, I will talk about the problem that immediately follows this design: once your traits and query builders carry a generic context parameter, how do you avoid falling into generic type gymnastics and monomorphization bloat?
